  - Letters rather than `[` and `]`, which need AltGr on a Canadian French layout and never reach `keyboard.ts`.
  - The keys and the list sort with one comparator (start, end, id), which is the order `activeHunks` renders in. Today they break ties three different ways.

**What each shape looks like afterwards.** Capsule, cup (a grey line with no fill) and dotted line are all 0.75em wide; [x] is a keycap and "bar" is a margin mark. A focused hunk of any of these shapes has its capsule and ghost text on `--focus-bg`, with the arrowhead in the rail. Word-sized hunks are unchanged apart from that arrowhead.

| Shape | Pending | Accepted | Dismissed |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Comma inserted after "Mira" (case 2) | caret in a purple capsule; `-> [,]`; bar | comma over a cup; `ADDED [,]` | grey caret over a dotted line; faded `-> [,]` |
| Comma or doubled space deleted | the comma or gap in a capsule; `-> DELETE` (plus `[SPACE]` for whitespace); bar | grey caret over a cup; `WAS [,]` or `WAS [SPACE]` | glyph over a dotted line; faded `-> DELETE` |
| One-character swap, "." to "," | period in a capsule; `-> [,]`; bar | comma over a cup; `WAS [.]` | period over a dotted line; faded `-> [,]` |
| Hyphen to space, "double standard" (case 1) | hyphen in a capsule; `-> [SPACE]`; bar | a cup under the gap, reaching the edges of "e" and "s"; `WAS [-]`, not struck through; row "- (hyphen) -> (space)" | hyphen over a dotted line; faded `-> [SPACE]` |
| One-letter word, "i" to "I" | "i" in a capsule; `-> [I]`; bar | "I" over a cup; `WAS [i]` | "i" over a dotted line; faded `-> [I]` |

A change whose live text holds a line break, such as a paragraph join, gets no capsule. Its keycap names what replaces the break, and while it is pending its bar marks the line.

**Tasks**, in order. `small.2` to `small.4` are pure and can land at any time after M2. The other tasks change what the window shows, so they land after `theme.4`.

- [ ] `small.1` **Caret.** In `app.css`, `.hunk__caret` becomes `display: inline` at `font-size: 0.8em` (still 2 x 15 px), coloured by status through `--ink-faint` and `--border-strong`. Its own commit. Repeat the window-width sweep in the real window: the caret must never detach from the word before it.
- [ ] `small.2` **DOM-text guard.** `test/renderer/hunkDom.test.ts` renders `HunkSpan` with `react-dom/server` for every shape and status in the table. It strips the `.hunk__above` subtree and asserts that what remains is exactly `hunkFaces(hunk).live`. It lands before steps 6 to 8 touch the markup, and it protects find, copy and `data-hunk-start/end`.
- [ ] `small.3` **Small predicates and names.** `src/shared/ui/small.ts` gets `isSmallText`, `isSmallLive`, `isSmallChange`, `whitespaceName`, `characterName`, `sideText` and `changeInContext`. `test/shared/small.test.ts` has tables over shape x status, plus a fast-check property through the real `trimCommonAffixes`: one punctuation or whitespace insertion, deletion or swap at a word boundary always trims to a small change, and one letter changed inside a word of two or more letters never does.
- [ ] `small.4` **Ghost content and flush flags.** `src/shared/render.ts` gets `ghostContent(hunk)`, and `segmentsIn` gets `flushBefore` and `flushAfter` on hunk segments (true when the neighbour is a hunk, or plain text under 2 code points). `test/shared/render.test.ts` covers the shape x status matrix and a property: the keycap text is the ghost side or its name, never the live face (except for an accepted insertion). Segment texts must still join up to the block slice.
- [ ] `small.5` **Text surfaces.**
  - `summaryLine` in `src/shared/ui/suggestions.ts` builds each side with `sideText`.
  - The pairs in `HunkTooltip.tsx`, and the details pair, stale pair and context chip in `SidePanel.tsx`, use the same names.
  - The aria-label in `HunkSpan.tsx` speaks the names.
  - Update the "(nothing)" assertions in `test/shared/ui.test.ts` on purpose, not as fallout.
- [ ] `small.6` **Capsule.**
  - `HunkSpan.tsx` adds the `hunk--small` and `hunk--flush-before/after` classes, and `app.css` gets the capsule rules.
  - `--tint-small: 16%` and `--tint-small-hover: 26%` go in both token blocks.
  - Set the dark `--tint-small` and `--tint-small-hover` by `theme.8`'s measure (11.3 e): the tinted box's WCAG contrast with what it sits on (`--bg`, `--para-active-bg` and `--focus-bg`), at rest and hovered, compared as a range across the seven categories, lowest with lowest and highest with highest, dark at least light. Compared category by category, spelling and homophone already come out up to 0.03 lower in dark at the shares `theme.8` kept, so a per-category gap under 0.04 is no reason to raise a share. Pin both values in `LIGHT_VALUES` and `DARK_VALUES` in `themeCss.test.ts` and in 11.3 (e), and look at the theme.3 driver's dark shots.
  - `test/renderer/themeCss.test.ts` gets one new pair: `--ink` on each `--cat-*` mixed at `--tint-small-hover` over `--bg` must reach 4.5:1.
  - Rerun theme.1's LayerTree probe with the isolation in place. `.center` must keep `lcd_text_disallowed_reason: none`; if it does not, isolate only the paragraphs that hold a small hunk.
  - Check the paragraph editor (`.para--editing` is also a `.para`), find highlights, the active ring and the ghost rows on a paragraph's first line.
- [ ] `small.7` **Keycap.**
  - First build a density fixture: a dialogue line with four pending and two accepted small changes. If the rows pile up, drop the border for punctuation and keep it only for named whitespace.
  - `HunkSpan.tsx` renders from `ghostContent`. The rules `.hunk__key`, `.hunk__key--name` and `.hunk__above--key { padding-bottom: 0.12em }` keep the row inside the gap between lines.
  - `clipGhosts` in `src/renderer/state/ghosts.ts` never cuts a ghost to the left of its key's right edge. That replaces the 22 px minimum, and the existing gap and line tolerance stay.
  - The "?" of an uncertain hunk stays after the key.
- [ ] `small.8` **Margin marks.**
  - `marginMarks(segments, focusedId)` and `markAnchorName(blockKey, index)` go in `src/shared/ui/marks.ts`, tested in `test/shared/marks.test.ts`: focus wins over pending, stale and hidden hunks are never marked, and names are valid dashed idents for any block key.
  - `ReviewPane.tsx` renders the marks as direct children of the `<p>`. `HunkSpan.tsx` takes a `markAnchor` prop, and `src/renderer/state/hover.ts` gains `hoverSoon`.
  - `app.css` gets the `.mark` rules and the 39 px rail. Check a merged "12-13" label in the narrower rail.
  - First confirm anchor positioning in the real window together with the capsule's isolation. The fallback is a pass that reads every rect first and then writes every position, reusing `marginMarks`.
- [ ] `small.9` **Keys.**
  - `stepFocus` and the shared comparator go in `src/shared/ui/navigate.ts`, tested in `test/shared/navigate.test.ts`. `groupSuggestions` uses the same comparator.
  - `moveFocus(delta, {pendingOnly, view})` in `store.ts`, keeping the clamp tests in `test/renderer/store.test.ts`.
  - `readingRange` in `src/renderer/state/scroll.ts` reads `data-start`/`data-end` on each paragraph.
  - `keyboard.ts` handles `J` and `K`, and the side panel's empty-state hint mentions Shift+J.
- [ ] `small.10` **Words.**
  - Legend items with swatches, added to the list `apply.4` rewrote (it landed first, on 2026-10-17, so these go between Dismissed and Not saved to chapter rather than in the same commit): the capsule, the keycap ("boxed when it is one character or a space", beside "struck through"), the margin bar, and Shift+J.
  - PLAN.md section 7: the insertion bullet, the role rule, a new "Small changes" bullet, margin marks, interactions, and the suggestions list.
  - Tick this workstream's checkbox and add an Appendix C entry, "Small changes are drawn bigger, never stored bigger", with the measurements.
- [ ] `small.11` **Verify in the real window (C.8).**
  - Add the table's shapes to theme.3's fixture, plus a word hunk with a touching comma and two small changes two words apart.
  - Shoot them in light and dark at 100% and 200% zoom, and at 80% for the keycap border.
  - At each zoom, repeat the hit scan (a click at every pixel over each shape), the caret width sweep and the check that each mark lines up with its line.
  - Send the author a contact sheet of paragraphs 74 and 76, before and after, in all three states, with four questions:
    - did case 1 bother them because the edit was hard to find again, or because "WAS -" was unreadable;
    - "period" or "full stop";
    - should clicking a bar accept the change instead of selecting it;
    - do the boxes stay calm on a page full of commas.

**Acceptance.**

- Paragraph 76, pending comma after "Mira":
  - while scrolling at reading speed, the spot after "Mira" shows as a tinted capsule, and a bar sits in the margin beside its line;
  - a click anywhere on the capsule accepts it;
  - the ghost reads "-> [,]" with the comma near reading size, and a click on the comma never dismisses it.
- Accepting the comma and then dismissing it leaves no purple in the line; dismissed, the caret is light grey.
- Paragraph 74, accepted "double standard": the ghost reads "WAS [-]" with the hyphen plainly a hyphen, the gap has a grey line about a letter wide, and the list row reads "- (hyphen) -> (space)".
- After scrolling to the middle of a chapter, Shift+J lands on the next pending change below the top of the screen, never on paragraph 1. With Caps Lock on, `j` still moves.
- Save to chapter on a chapter with a dozen small accepted changes writes exactly those changes, and the chapter's git diff shows nothing else. Copy result matches, and Ctrl+F and a copied paragraph show no extra text.
- At 200% zoom and in dark mode, capsules, keycaps and bars line up with their text. Word-sized hunks look as before, apart from the focus arrowhead.
- `npm test`, `npm run lint` and `npm run build` pass, including the new guard, predicate, ghost, marks, navigation and contrast tests.

**Not doing.**

- **Widening stored hunks or setting a minimum stored width.** That is a data change, not a visual one (first decision).
- **Showing a pending comma in the line.** The legend promises that the line is what Save would write, and the save workstream is built on "The chapter already matches the page".
- **Padding or a minimum inline width on tiny text.** It reflows the line under the pointer on every click and moves span rects and margin notes.
- **Underlining the neighbouring word and making it clickable.**
  - It misstates the change: "ADDED" over an underlined "Mira," reads as "Mira was added".
  - It needs a measuring pass on every accept and a contrast exception below 3:1.
  - It stays a fallback only if the author finds the capsule too small, and then for paint only.
- **A fixed-width dab centred on the change.** The capsule does the same job, painted beneath the text.
- **A blue focus ring and an arrival pulse.** The ring uses the comment blue, the pulse is motion, and the arrowhead already marks where focus lands. The yellow focus on the active paragraph's yellow (1.05:1) goes to theme.8, which gave it an `--active-ring` hairline inside the same 2 px (11.3 e).
- **Symbol glyphs for whitespace, in the line or in the ghost.** An interpunct reads as punctuation, and a real glyph in the line would be found by Ctrl+F and copied.
- **Grey margin marks for decided changes on paragraph hover.** They flicker as paragraphs pass under a resting pointer, and they promote layers in the scroller that theme.1 made LCD-clean. Category colour on decided tiny hunks is left out too, so a worked chapter stays quiet.
- **Moving or disabling the x (C.34), a "small only" filter chip or key, and minimap marks sized by width (C.17).**

**Dependencies.**

- **theme.4.** The light baseline must stay pixel-identical through theme.4, so every visible step lands after it and, when it lands, adds itself to the list of deliberate light differences in `theme.4`'s tick (M5): caret colour, capsule, keycap, marks, list chip, rail width. These steps use theme.4's `--focus-bg` and `--tint-*` tokens.
- **theme.5.** `--tint-small` and `--tint-small-hover` go into both token blocks and the contrast test. There is no new colour token: marks reuse `--cat-*`, `--ink`, `--ink-faint` and `--border-strong`, and nothing here uses the save workstream's ember `--unsaved` colour.
- **theme.1, theme.3 and theme.8.**
  - theme.1: its LCD probe is rerun with the isolation in place.
  - theme.3: its fixture and driver are reused for this work.
  - theme.8: it owns `--focus-bg` against `--para-active-bg`, settled with a hairline (11.3 e). It landed before `--tint-small` existed, so `small.6` sets the dark `--tint-small` and `--tint-small-hover` itself, by theme.8's measure as `small.6`'s own bullet states it (the range across the categories, dark at least light), checked by eye in the theme.3 driver's dark shots. The guess here was 22 to 30%, but `--tint-pending` and `--tint-hover` came out as strong in dark as in light at the same share, so the light 16% may do.
- **11.6 question 9** (`--ink-faint` to #8a8378, 2.8 to 3.4:1) decides the grey of case 1's line and its "was" ghost. This workstream does not decide it a second time.
- **apply.4 (save workstream).** The legend is rewritten once, with this workstream's lines next to apply.12's. `apply.4` did the rewrite before any of this workstream had landed, so `small.10` adds its items to that list and does not rewrite it again.
- **Appendix C.** The new entry takes the next free number at commit time, as the rules at the top of section 11 say.
- **Line numbers.** `HunkSpan.tsx` and `app.css` changed while this was designed (the hidden "insertion" word was removed in C.37), so implementation cites selectors and functions, not line numbers.

## Appendix B: development machine (2026-09-08, updated 2026-10-16)

Windows 11, Node 24.21.0, npm 11.19.0, git 2.45.2. Claude Code 2.1.266 is installed natively at `%USERPROFILE%\.local\bin\claude.exe`. `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` is not set. PowerShell 5.1 is the primary shell and Git Bash is available. Shell heredocs are unreliable on this machine: long ones fail, and escape handling has turned a backslash sequence into a control character. Files are therefore written with the editor tools, not shell redirection. The folder is a git repository. Since 2026-10-16 it is public at `github.com/MichaelBradetLegris/lapidary` (remote `origin`), default branch `main`, which began as one fresh commit of the tree (`opensource.9`); the earlier private repository keeps the old history. The folder has a repo-local identity, `Michael Bradet-Legris <45394569+MichaelBradetLegris@users.noreply.github.com>`, and `commit.gpgsign false`, so no public commit carries the personal address or a key tied to it. Under Appendix D 7 as changed on 2026-10-14, the agent may commit and push: it works on a branch per milestone, pushes it, and fast-forwards `main` once the milestone verifies. It never force-pushes, and the `main` ruleset refuses force-pushes and deletion anyway.

## Appendix C: deviations from this plan, recorded as they happened

### C.1 Vite 7 instead of Vite 8 (Phase 0)

electron-vite 5.0.0, the newest stable release, declares `vite: ^5 || ^6 || ^7` as a peer dependency. Vite 8 is only reachable through electron-vite 6.0.0-beta.1, and `@vitejs/plugin-react` 6 requires Vite 8 while version 5 covers Vite 4 to 7. The project therefore uses electron-vite 5.0.0 with Vite 7.3.6 and `@vitejs/plugin-react` 5.2.0, all stable releases. Nothing in the design depends on a Vite 8 feature. Revisit when electron-vite 6 ships stable.

### C.2 ESM main process, CJS preload (Phase 0)

`@anthropic-ai/claude-agent-sdk` is ESM only (`"type": "module"`, `main: sdk.mjs`). Requiring it from a CommonJS Electron main bundle would depend on Electron's `require(esm)` support, which the plan never verified. The package is therefore `"type": "module"`, electron-vite emits `out/main/index.js` as ESM, and the preload is pinned to CommonJS (`out/preload/index.cjs`) so the window can keep `sandbox: true`. Paths in main use `import.meta.url` rather than `__dirname`.

### C.3 Typecheck runs through project references (Phase 0)

`tsc --noEmit -p ...` cannot be combined with `composite`, so `npm run typecheck` is `tsc --build --force` over `tsconfig.node.json` and `tsconfig.web.json`, both `emitDeclarationOnly` into `node_modules/.tmp`. Behaviour is the same: type errors fail the command, nothing lands in `out/`.

### C.4 Matches must respect word boundaries (Phase 1)

Sections 4.3 and 4.5 anchor on plain substring search. The property tests found this is not safe: "began" matches inside "beganning" and, in the generated cases, "w1" matches inside "w10", so a hunk could anchor to the wrong span and rewrite untouched prose. `cleanOccurrences` in `src/shared/diff/fuzzy.ts` now prefers matches whose neighbouring characters are not word characters, falling back to raw matches only when a word occurs solely mid-word. It also decides `exact` versus `ambiguous`.

Rebase gained a related change: instead of "look for the expected side, then the other side", it collects candidates for both sides and ranks them by word-boundary cleanliness first, then by distance from the old offset, with a one-character tie-break in favour of the side the hunk expected. Without this, an accepted hunk whose `original` still appears mid-word elsewhere re-anchored onto the wrong text.

### C.5 Rebase adopts what is on disk (Phase 1)

Section 4.5 says to update `baseSide` when the author applied a change by hand, but not what happens to the decision. Leaving a hunk `pending` while `baseSide` is `replacement` would make the next save silently revert the author's own edit. The rule implemented is: when the found side differs from the recorded one, the disk wins and the decision follows it. Text now showing the replacement becomes `accepted`; an `accepted` hunk whose text is back to the original returns to `pending`. When the found side matches the recorded side, which is the normal case, the decision is untouched, so the round-trip property in 4.5 still holds. Since `apply.23` the disk wins only where it plainly says so, and a hunk it seems to contradict, but not plainly, leaves the page instead (C.52).

### C.6 Rebase ranks candidates by surrounding context (Phase 3)

Ranking re-anchor candidates by distance from the old offset breaks on short common words. Driving the real app showed it: after a paragraph was inserted above them, an accepted `of` -> `have` hunk re-anchored onto an unrelated `of` and its decision was flipped back to pending. `rebase.ts` now scores every candidate by how many characters either side of it still agree with the old file (40 each way) and ranks word-boundary cleanliness first, then context agreement, then distance, then the expected side. There is a regression test for it in `test/shared/rebase.test.ts`. `apply.23` (C.52) adds zero-width places for a side that holds no text, counts the start and end of the file as agreeing where both texts begin or end there, and puts the expected side before distance.

### C.7 The guidelines file is not a chapter (Phase 3)

`GUIDELINES.md` lives in the manuscript folder and is Markdown, so it appeared in the chapter list and could be opened and reviewed. `files:list` now excludes the resolved guidelines path.

### C.8 How the running app is tested

electron-vite's dev command honours a `REMOTE_DEBUGGING_PORT` environment variable, so `REMOTE_DEBUGGING_PORT=9222 npm run dev` exposes the renderer over the DevTools protocol without any code in the app. A short script can then evaluate expressions in the live window, including `await import('/state/store.ts')`, which returns the very store module the app is running. That is how the file list, save, watcher and rebase behaviour above were exercised against real files. No debugging hooks are compiled into the app.

### C.9 Tools are removed with `tools: []`, not a hardcoded deny list (Phase 4, risk 2)

`sdk.d.ts` has a top-level `tools` option documented as "`[]` (empty array) - Disable all built-in tools". That is used instead of enumerating `disallowedTools`, so the list can never drift as tool names change. The call also passes `mcpServers: {}`, `strictMcpConfig: true`, `settingSources: []`, `persistSession: false`, `permissionMode: 'dontAsk'` and `cwd: app.getPath('userData')`. `Options.env` REPLACES the subprocess environment rather than merging, so `process.env` is spread explicitly.

### C.10 `maxTurns` is 3, not 1 (Phase 4, risk 1)

The SDK delivers structured output through an internal end-turn tool call and retries on a schema mismatch, so one turn is not a safe budget. The value is 3 as the plan's risk note anticipated. The verified run used one turn of it.

### C.11 The zod JSON Schema target is `draft-07` (Phase 4)

Section 5.3 writes `z.toJSONSchema(..., { target: 'draft-7' })`. zod 4.5.4 accepts `'draft-04' | 'draft-07' | 'draft-2020-12' | 'openapi-3.0'`. The code uses `'draft-07'` and a test asserts the emitted `$schema`.

### C.12 The model dropdown keeps aliases the CLI does not list (Phase 4, risk 5)

`supportedModels()` on the author's Max plan returns `default`, `opus[1m]`, `claude-fable-5-1[1m]`, `sonnet`, `haiku`. Plain `opus`, which is the default setting and which the CLI resolves correctly to `claude-opus-5`, is not among them, so a plain `<select>` displayed the wrong row. `modelOptions` in `src/renderer/state/models.ts` prepends the stored value when the list does not contain it.

### C.13 Risk 3 is closed: the SDK runs its bundled binary from Electron main

_Superseded by C.42 (2026-10-14): the app no longer uses the SDK or bundles Claude Code._

No `ELECTRON_RUN_AS_NODE` and no `pathToClaudeCodeExecutable` were needed. `checkRuntime()` found `node_modules/@anthropic-ai/claude-agent-sdk-win32-x64/claude.exe` and reported `state: 'ready'` with the account email and `Claude Max`. `pathToClaudeCodeExecutable` is passed only when the bundled binary is absent and a native install is found instead.

### C.14 A dev-only userData override (Phase 5a)

The author's saved settings point at their real vault, so a test run must not read them. `src/main/index.ts` honours `MANUSCRIPT_REVIEW_USER_DATA` and calls `app.setPath('userData', ...)` at module scope, before anything can read the path. Since `hardening.1` only development builds honour the variable; a packaged build takes `--isolated-user-data=<dir>` instead (C.39). Verification runs use `.dev-userdata` in the repo (gitignored) with settings pointing at `samples/`. Nothing in the app depends on the variable being set.

### C.15 Find uses the CSS Custom Highlight API, not `findInPage` or `<mark>` (Phase 5a, item 11)

Wrapping matches in elements would split the text nodes inside hunk spans, which is exactly the structure the offsets and the absolutely positioned ghosts depend on. `webContents.findInPage` avoids that but cannot be scoped, so it would count and highlight ghost text as if it were the chapter. `CSS.highlights` paints ranges over the existing nodes: no DOM change at all, and the tree walker skips `.hunk__ghost`, so the count only refers to real prose. `src/renderer/state/find.ts` degrades to counting and scrolling without painting if the API is ever missing.

### C.16 Bulk actions only move pending suggestions (Phase 5a, item 8)

The old `setAllStatuses` overwrote every hunk, so "Dismiss all" silently threw away work the author had already accepted. The confirmation wording the author asked for ("Suggestions you already accepted are not changed") is now the actual rule: accept and dismiss target only `pending` hunks, and reset targets only decided ones. `bulkPlan` returns the affected ids with the sentence, and the store applies exactly those ids, so the count in the modal cannot drift from what happens.

### C.17 The position strip approximates by character offset (Phase 5a, item 14)

The marks beside the scrollbar are placed at `hunk.start / base.text.length`. Prose sets at a constant line height, so character position tracks vertical position closely enough for a locator, and the alternative, measuring every hunk element on every reflow, is not worth the cost for a hint. Clicking a mark focuses and scrolls to the real element, so the approximation never decides anything.

### C.18 The "Ã—" glyph was in the source, not in the pipeline (Phase 5a, item 1)

`App.tsx` contained the characters `Ã` (U+00C3) and `—` (U+2014) where a multiplication sign was meant: someone's editor had read UTF-8 `C3 97` as Windows-1252 and saved the result. The file itself is valid UTF-8, which is why the chips' `×` rendered correctly and only this one did not. Rather than fix the character, every control glyph in the renderer is now an inline SVG path in `src/renderer/components/icons.tsx`, which no encoding round trip can corrupt.

### C.19 Dev shortcuts open DevTools detached (Phase 5a, item 10)

With the application menu removed, F12, Ctrl+Shift+I and Ctrl+R are handled in `before-input-event` and only when the app is not packaged. `toggleDevTools()` did not open a window on this machine; explicit `isDevToolsOpened()` plus `openDevTools({ mode: 'detach' })` does, and a detached window leaves the manuscript at full width. Note for future test rounds: CDP `Input.dispatchKeyEvent` does not reach `before-input-event`, and `SendKeys` does not deliver `{F12}`; real key presses through `keybd_event` do.

### C.20 The packaged app must be handed the unpacked binary (Phase 5b, item 1; risk 4)

_Superseded by C.42 (2026-10-14): nothing of Anthropic's is packaged any more._

`asarUnpack` alone was not enough. `bundledBinary()` looked in `app.getAppPath()` first, which once packaged is `resources/app.asar`, and Electron's fs shim answers `existsSync` for an unpacked file using its **archive** path. So the check passed, the asar path was handed to the SDK, and the spawn failed with a misleading "binary exists but failed to launch ... this usually means the binary does not match this system's libc". The packaged app reported `state: 'error'` with a message about musl on a Windows machine.

Two changes fixed it. `bundledBinary()` now rejects any candidate inside an asar outright and looks in `resources/app.asar.unpacked` first, and `resolvedExecutable()` passes the located path to the SDK explicitly whenever the app is packaged, rather than leaving the SDK to resolve its own sibling package with `require.resolve` (which would land inside the archive for the same reason). Development is unchanged: the SDK still finds its own binary, as C.13 recorded. The config unpacks both `@anthropic-ai/claude-agent-sdk-win32-x64/**` and `@anthropic-ai/claude-agent-sdk/**`.

### C.21 The app name is read out of the TypeScript source by two build scripts (Phase 5b, item 1)

`APP_NAME` lives in `src/shared/branding.ts`, which main and renderer import directly and `electron.vite.config.ts` substitutes into `index.html` through a `transformIndexHtml` plugin. The two consumers that cannot import TypeScript, `package.json` and the electron-builder config, read the constant out of that file with a regex in `scripts/branding.cjs`; `scripts/sync-branding.cjs` writes it into `package.json`'s `productName` and runs automatically as `prepackage`. It is not elegant, but it means renaming the app is a single edit, which is what the author asked for. Since `opensource.4` (2026-10-16) the same script also writes `repository`, `homepage` and `bugs` from `APP_REPOSITORY`. `npm run branding:check` runs it with `--check`, which writes nothing and exits 0 when package.json is in sync, or lists each field that differs and exits 1.

`APP_DATA_DIR` in the same file is deliberately **not** the display name. Electron derives `userData` from the application name, so tying the two together would move the author's settings and orphan every review in the default reviews folder the first time the app was renamed. `src/main/index.ts` pins `userData` to `%APPDATA%/ai-grammar-checker` unless `MANUSCRIPT_REVIEW_USER_DATA` overrides it.

### C.22 The icon is generated, not drawn by hand (Phase 5b, item 1)

`scripts/make-icons.py` holds one geometry spec in fractions of the icon's side and emits both `resources/icon/icon.svg` and every raster from it, so the vector and the bitmaps cannot drift. Rasters are drawn at 8x and reduced with LANCZOS, which is what keeps the 16 px version legible. Pillow writes the multi-size `.ico` directly. The art is name-neutral on purpose: a line of type, the coloured dotted underline this app puts under a pending suggestion, and the faint ghost line above it. An icon built from a letter would have to be redrawn with every rename.

### C.23 Superseded suggestions need a flag, not just a status (Phase 5b, item 3)

A suggestion swallowed by the author's own edit is dismissed and unanchored, but that is not enough on its own: its words usually still exist inside the edit that replaced them, so the next rebase re-anchors it happily, on top of the manual hunk, and `resolveOverlaps` then has to demote one of them on every reload. `Hunk.supersededBy` records which manual hunk took it, and `rebaseReview` leaves such a hunk stale as long as that hunk is still in the review. This is one field beyond what the feedback asked for, and it is the thing that makes "they leave the inline view and stay in the Unanchored section" true rather than usually true.

### C.24 Ghost collisions are solved by measuring, not by CSS (Phase 5b, item 2)

Whether two ghosts overlap depends on where the line broke, which is known only after layout. `clipGhosts` resets every ghost's max width, measures the rows, groups them by top, and clips any ghost that would run into the next one on the same line. Because ghosts are absolutely positioned, changing their width moves nothing the prose depends on, so the `ResizeObserver` that triggers the pass cannot be re-triggered by it.

### C.25 OpenAI effort is passed through, not folded down (Phase 5c)

The brief expected `xhigh` and `max` to be mapped to the highest effort the API accepts. The installed SDK's `ReasoningEffort` type is `'none' | 'minimal' | 'low' | 'medium' | 'high' | 'xhigh' | 'max'`, so the highest accepted value is the value itself and `OPENAI_EFFORT_FOR` is the identity. It is still written as an explicit `Record<EffortLevel, OpenAiEffort>` so a new app effort level fails to compile until someone decides what it means to OpenAI. The dialog says all five levels are passed through and that not every model supports them; a 400 from a model that refuses one says so.

### C.26 `generator.provider` is filled by its own load step, not by `migrateReview` (Phase 5c)

`migrateReview` has a tested contract from Phase 5b: a review that needs no rename comes back as the same object. Filling `provider: 'claude'` inside it would have broken that contract for every pre-5c Claude review. The default lives in `withGeneratorProvider`, and `reviews.ts` runs `upgradeLoadedReview` (both steps, in order) on every file it reads. OpenAI reviews also get `kind: 'openai'`, so the history badge and anything else keyed on `kind` tell the two apart without reading `provider`. Hunks keep `source: 'claude'` for both providers: it is the "a model wrote this" marker the editing rules key on, and renaming it would invalidate every review on disk.

### C.27 The strict schema is derived, and its nulls are undone precisely (Phase 5c)

OpenAI's strict mode needs every property required and has no notion of an absent optional field, so the one optional property in the schema (a comment's `quote`) becomes required and nullable in the schema sent to OpenAI. A blanket "drop every null" on the way back would also drop a comment's `paragraph: null`, which is required and means "about the whole chapter", and zod would reject the reply. `stripOptionalNulls` walks the reply alongside the original schema and removes a null only where the original did not require the property. Constraint keywords strict mode has historically rejected (`minLength`, `maxLength`, `minimum`, `maximum`, and so on) are dropped from the OpenAI copy only; zod still enforces them locally, and Claude's schema is untouched.

### C.28 A model setting per provider (Phase 5c)

One `model` setting shared by both providers would have sent `opus` to OpenAI the first time the author switched. `openaiModel` sits beside `model`, which keeps its name because that is what every settings.json on disk already says. An empty `openaiModel` means "the list's default", which main resolves with the same `defaultOpenAiModel` the dialog uses, so what the dialog shows is what is sent.

### C.29 The OpenAI model list is fetched lazily (Phase 5c)

Claude's list still arrives with the launch-time runtime check. OpenAI's needs a network call on the author's key, so it is fetched the first time the review dialog shows OpenAI, and again whenever the key changes. The OpenAI runtime check itself only reads the local secrets file.

### C.30 Timers in a hidden test window (verification note)

While driving the dev window over CDP, the page reported `document.visibilityState === 'hidden'` part way through (the cause was not established; the window was not minimised), and Chromium's background timer throttling slowed `setTimeout`-based waits in the test script to a crawl. The driver's waits now spin on `MessageChannel`, which is not throttled. Nothing in the app changed; this is for future test rounds.

### C.31 Blocks merge for a hunk that starts inside the blank line (Phase 5d)

`buildRenderBlocks` used to merge two paragraphs into one block only when a hunk crossed the end of the first paragraph's text. A deletion of the last paragraph from its editor starts in the blank line before it, so it crossed no paragraph's text and belonged to neither block: composed correctly, drawn nowhere. The rule is now "a hunk starts before the next paragraph and ends after this block", which includes the old case. Deletions from the editor otherwise take the blank line after the paragraph, exactly as the full-text diff does.

### C.32 The status card lives outside the scroller (Phase 5d)

It was first a sticky card at the top of the pane. The pane is a stretched flex item, one screen tall, with its content overflowing it, so the sticky card scrolled away after the first screen. It now floats at the bottom of the pane's non-scrolling wrapper, like the notification cards at its top-right.

### C.33 The find bar lives outside the scroller (Phase 5e)

The same trap as C.32. The bar was sticky inside the pane, which is one screen tall with its content overflowing, so more than a screen down it opened out of sight and Ctrl+F seemed to do nothing. While it was open out of sight, every redraw also scrolled back to the active match, because the scroll ran on every repaint rather than only on a request. It is now a strip above the scroller, and scrolling is tied to `find.seek`, which only typing and stepping advance and which never goes back to zero.

### C.34 The x is invisible, not absent, until the suggestion is hovered (Phase 5e)

With `display: none`, an x that sat past the end of its word took no space, so a pointer arriving where it would appear found nothing to hover and the x never came back. It now keeps its room at zero opacity and is out of the tab order (the keyboard dismisses with `d`). Ghost clipping reserves that room when it shortens a ghost.

### C.35 Keys and wheel sent over DevTools skip Electron's input hooks (verification note, Phase 5f)

Key presses and wheel events injected with `Input.dispatchKeyEvent` / `Input.dispatchMouseEvent` reach the page but never pass through `before-input-event` or produce `zoom-changed`, so the zoom shortcuts cannot be tested that way. They were tested with real input sent through Windows (`keybd_event`, `mouse_event`) to the app's own window, after checking it was in the foreground. Real key presses arrive as `keyDown`.

### C.36 The window shows on first paint, page load or a timer, whichever comes first (packaging fix, 2026-10-14)

The window is created hidden and shown on `ready-to-show`, so it never flashes blank. In the packaged build on the author's machine that event never arrived: the process ran, the page loaded and rendered (checked over DevTools), and the window stayed hidden, so the installed app seemed not to open. Forcing `ShowWindow` on it brought up a working app. `did-finish-load` and a 3-second timer are now fallbacks in `createWindow`, and whichever fires first shows the window, once. The rebuilt package showed its window 0.6 s after launch. Development was never affected.

Verification note: `npm run package` fails with `EBUSY ... unlink dist\win-unpacked\resources\app.asar` while VS Code holds that file open (the Restart Manager API named VS Code as the holder). Restarting VS Code releases it.

### C.37 A copy from the page is the chapter's plain text (2026-10-14)

The author copied a paragraph and the paste contained the word "insertion". It was a visually hidden label inside every insertion, and the find bar matched it too. A real copy also showed more: the plain text had a blank line in front of every suggestion, because each ghost row is a box of its own, and the HTML carried the app's markup. The hidden word is gone; "insertion" is now part of the suggestion's `aria-label`. The `.prose` element handles `copy` itself and writes only `text/plain`: the chapter text the selection covers, as the page currently reads (pending suggestions show the original, accepted ones the replacement), with a blank line between paragraphs. `NOT_PROSE` in `renderer/state/prose.ts` is the one list of decoration that find and copy both skip, and the join is `joinProsePieces` in `shared/ui/copy.ts`. A copy inside an open paragraph editor is left to the textarea.

### C.38 Renamed to Lapidary: what moved and what did not (`brand.1`, 2026-10-14)

**What moved.** `APP_NAME` is now `Lapidary`, and with it:

- the window and HTML titles;
- package.json's `productName` (through `sync-branding.cjs`);
- the installer and shortcut names;
- the README title.

The package name went from `ai-grammar-checker` to `manuscript-review`, the frozen name the installer folder and the updater cache will use (11.1). `APP_ID` (the unchanged `dev.bradetlegris.manuscript-review`) and `APP_REPOSITORY` (`MichaelBradetLegris/lapidary`) joined `APP_NAME` in `src/shared/branding.ts`. `scripts/branding.cjs` now reads any constant there by name, so the builder config takes `appId` from it instead of repeating the string. The Claude client tag is `<app name in lower case>/<version>`. The OpenAI dev variable is `MANUSCRIPT_REVIEW_OPENAI_BASE_URL`, a prefix that never changes with the name.

**What did not move.** `APP_DATA_DIR` stays `ai-grammar-checker`, so settings, the encrypted OpenAI key and reviews in the default folder stay where they are (C.21). The review files' `SCHEMA_NAME` is unchanged, so existing reviews load as before. `APP_ID` did not move either, so an installed "Stet" from an earlier build has the same NSIS GUID (`NsisTarget.js` derives it from appId when `nsis.guid` is unset) and the same install and uninstall registry keys (`multiUser.nsh`). The Lapidary installer therefore treats Stet as an older version of itself: it starts from Stet's install folder, runs Stet's uninstaller silently with `/KEEP_APP_DATA --updated` (`installSection.nsh`, `uninstallOldVersion` in `installUtil.nsh`), and Installed apps afterwards shows a single entry, Lapidary. The data folder they share is untouched. Today's assisted installer adds a `Lapidary` subfolder to the folder it starts from (`instFilesPre` in `assistedInstaller.nsh`), so it lands in `Programs\Stet\Lapidary`; the one-click installer of 11.3 (n) would use Stet's folder as it is. This is read from the electron-builder 26.15.3 templates; no install of a Stet build followed by a Lapidary build has been run yet. (Corrected on 2026-10-16: this section first said Stet was a different app to Windows and had to be uninstalled by hand.)

### C.39 Dev switches are ignored by packaged builds (`hardening.1`, 2026-10-14)

Every development switch is read in `src/main/devSwitches.ts`, and nowhere else reads one from `process.env`. The switches are `MANUSCRIPT_REVIEW_USER_DATA`, `MANUSCRIPT_REVIEW_OPENAI_BASE_URL`, `MANUSCRIPT_REVIEW_DEV_NO_CLAUDE_CODE` (renamed by C.43), `MANUSCRIPT_REVIEW_DEV_FAKE_UPDATE` and `MREV_VERBOSE`. A packaged build ignores them all and keeps their names for the log. A variable left in a shell profile could otherwise have sent the stored OpenAI key to another server, or moved an installed app's settings. The one switch a packaged build honours is the `--isolated-user-data=<dir>` command-line flag: it has to be typed for each launch, so it cannot linger, and it is how packaged builds are tested without touching real settings. It also wins over the variable in development.

The OpenAI SDK fills its base URL, organization, project and admin key from `OPENAI_*` variables whenever an option is left out. `clientOptions` therefore sets all of them, and a test builds a real client with hostile values in every one of those variables. A packaged build also sets the SDK's `logLevel` to `off`. `OPENAI_CUSTOM_HEADERS` is still read by the SDK, but a header cannot change where the request goes.

### C.40 One running copy per user data folder (`release.18`, 2026-10-14)

Two copies of the app on the same data folder would race on `settings.json` and the review files. `src/main/index.ts` takes `app.requestSingleInstanceLock()` right after setting userData, and Chromium keys the lock on that folder. The copy that loses quits before it writes anything. The one already running restores, shows and focuses its window on `second-instance`.

A test run with its own scratch folder still starts beside the author's copy. Checked with real launches: a second launch on the same folder exited in 0.4 s with code 0 and brought the minimised first window back, and a launch on another folder ran alongside.

`src/main/providers/controllers.ts` tracks every AbortController that work in flight holds: reviews, and for now the Claude runtime check and the model list. `will-quit` aborts them all synchronously and stops the watcher. That replaces the old `before-quit` and `window-all-closed` calls to `stopWatching()`. A packaged Windows build sets its AUMID to `APP_ID`, so a pinned shortcut and the running window share a taskbar button.

### C.41 The window refuses navigation and file drops (`opensource.12`, 2026-10-14)

The page is the whole app, and anything that replaced it would run with the preload bridge. Before this change, `location.href = 'https://example.com'` over CDP replaced the app outright (the negative control). Now `will-navigate` asks `navigationAction` in `src/main/navigation.ts` what to do:

- **allow** a reload of the page itself, fragment ignored;
- **external** for any other http or https address, which opens in the system browser instead;
- **block** everything else, including `file:`, `data:` and `javascript:`.

The handler reads `details.url`, because the url argument is deprecated in Electron 44. `will-attach-webview` is refused. `webSecurity` and `allowRunningInsecureContent` are stated at their safe defaults. The CSP gains `object-src 'none'; base-uri 'none'; form-action 'none'; frame-src 'none'`.

In the renderer, `dragover` and `drop` are stopped only when the drag carries files, so a file dropped on the window cannot replace the app and lose open paragraph boxes, while dragging text inside the page still works.

Checked in the rebuilt app:

- it renders under the new CSP;
- a synthetic file drop is stopped and a text drop is not;
- `location.href = 'file:///C:/Windows/win.ini'` leaves the app in place.

The web-address branch is unit-tested only, because triggering it opens a browser tab. A real drag from Explorer onto an open paragraph box is left to the author.

### C.42 Claude is the user's own `claude` command, not the SDK (`cli.1`, `setup.1a`, 2026-10-14)

Under Appendix D 8 and 21 the app ships nothing of Anthropic's. `@anthropic-ai/claude-agent-sdk` and its two peers, `@anthropic-ai/sdk` and `@modelcontextprotocol/sdk`, are gone from the dependencies. The builder config no longer unpacks anything, and the app runs the Claude Code each user installs, found at `%USERPROFILE%\.local\bin\claude.exe` or on PATH.

`src/main/providers/claudeCli.ts` speaks the same newline-delimited JSON over stdin and stdout that the SDK spoke:

- an `initialize` control request, which carries `systemPrompt: [text]` and `jsonSchema` and is answered with the model list and the account;
- the chapter as one user message;
- then `system`, `stream_event`, `assistant`, `rate_limit_event` and `result` messages, read exactly as before.

Everything was checked on 2026-10-14 against the author's installed Claude Code 2.1.266, with a cleaned environment:

- `initialize` returned the models and the account and sent no prompt;
- a Haiku probe and a review of samples Chapter 02 through the rebuilt app returned structured output (the review found the seeded "would of"), with cost and model usage;
- no `claude.exe` was left behind after the app quit.

Deviations from 11.3 (c) and (q):

- **System prompt and schema.** They go in `initialize` rather than in `--system-prompt` and `--json-schema`, so a long guidelines file cannot meet the Windows command-line limit.
- **Client variable.** `buildClaudeEnv` sets no `CLAUDE_AGENT_SDK_CLIENT_APP`, because the app is not the SDK.
- **`CLAUDE_CODE_SIMPLE`.** It is removed from the child environment: it is the switch behind `--bare`, which turns off subscription sign-in. A dev run started from inside a Claude Code session has it set, which is one reason verification runs need the cleaned environment of `setup.15`.

The runtime check now costs no usage. It runs `--version` and `auth status --json`, keeping only `loggedIn`, `authMethod`, `apiProvider` and `subscriptionType`, then the `initialize` exchange; the old check sent a real prompt. The model list reuses that answer for ten minutes.

`MIN_CLAUDE_CODE` in `src/shared/ai/claudeStatus.ts` is 2.1.266, the version the flags were verified against; an older copy is reported with `claude update` as the fix. The SDK's usage-limit prefixes were copied into the same file. `test/helpers/fakeClaude.ts` replays the protocol for `test/main/claudeCli.test.ts`, covering:

- success, malformed lines, a failure reported as a success with `is_error`, a usage limit, a refusal, an assistant error, the wrong output shape, a rejected flag, an early end, Cancel and quitting;
- the argument list, pinned.

The cost of this route: the model list depends on the user's copy of Claude Code. The author's 2.1.266 still lists Opus 5 as the default; `claude update` brings Opus 5.5. A future Claude Code could also change the protocol, which `release.10`'s per-release check covers.

### C.43 Verification runs use a cleaned environment (`setup.15`, 2026-10-14)

`npm run dev:clean` (`scripts/dev-clean.cjs`) starts the app with every variable named `CLAUDE*`, `ANTHROPIC*` or `DISABLE_*` removed, and prints their names but never their values. A shell inside a Claude Code session carries about thirty of them. Some change what the app's own Claude Code child does: `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` routes it through the session's proxy, and `CLAUDE_CODE_SIMPLE` turns off subscription sign-in.

The launcher always uses a scratch userData (`.dev-userdata`, `--user-data <dir>`, or `--user-data-fresh` for a first run) and DevTools on port 9222. Its other flags:

- `--claude-config-empty` or `--claude-config <dir>`: point `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` at an empty or given folder;
- `--api-key-dummy`: set a dummy `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`;
- `--no-claude-code`: behave as if Claude Code were not installed;
- `--exe <path>`: run a packaged build with `--isolated-user-data`.

The last switch, `MANUSCRIPT_REVIEW_DEV_NO_CLAUDE_CODE`, replaces `..._DEV_NO_BUNDLED_CLI`: since C.42 there is no bundled copy to skip, and "not installed" is the state the Setup screen must be tested in. In a development build, `claude.ts` warns once, in the terminal and the log, when `CLAUDECODE`, `CLAUDE_CODE_USER_EMAIL` or `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` is set.

Checked from inside a Claude Code session: the launcher printed and removed the session's variables, the app reported `not-signed-in` with `--claude-config-empty`, and that folder's new `.claude.json` held no `oauthAccount`.

### C.44 Comments follow their paragraphs through a save (`files.21`, 2026-10-15)

A comment knows its paragraph only by number. `markSaved` folded the decisions into a new base and moved every hunk's offsets, but left comment numbers and hunk labels as they were. A save that split, joined or deleted a paragraph therefore shifted every later comment. The author split 42-43 with the pen, and his comments marked 63 and 77 ended up quoting text in 64 and 78.

`remapAfterCommit` in `src/shared/review.ts` maps each old paragraph's span through the committed hunks. A comment follows its paragraph; when the paragraph was split, it goes with the part that holds its quote; when the quote moved elsewhere, it goes to the nearest occurrence by position. Hunk labels are recomputed from their new offsets.

`repairCommentAnchors`, run by `upgradeLoadedReview` on every load, mends reviews saved before the fix: a comment whose quote is missing from its paragraph moves to the nearest paragraph that holds it. One whose quote is found nowhere stays put, and an untouched review keeps its identity.

Checked with unit tests (split, joins, deletes, a fast-check property over random splits and joins, the repair) and in a dev window: splitting paragraph 1 of samples Chapter 01 with the pen and saving moved its paragraph-10 comment to 11, where its quote now sits.

### C.45 Reviews are bound to their source path and follow moved or renamed chapters (`files.1`, 2026-10-15)

Reviews were filed by a slug of the chapter's file name and the newest file in the folder was taken as the chapter's review. Two chapters with the same name in different folders therefore shared one review, and a Save in one could write the other's accepted changes into it. A renamed chapter lost its review entirely.

A review now belongs to the file its `sourcePath` names, compared with `samePath`. `listReviews`, `loadLatestReview` and `reviewIndex` (now keyed by the chapter paths main lists, with summaries cached by modification time and size) only see belonging reviews. The current review is the one with the latest `decidedAt ?? createdAt`; `decidedAt` is set by the store on every decision, comment status change and save, and never by opening a review. `saveReview` writes an explicit path only when it is a `.review.json` directly in the right folder and holds no other chapter's review; anything else gets a new entry, `<stamp>-2.review.json` when the stamp is taken.

When nothing belongs to a chapter that opens, `findAdoptable` looks for a chapter path whose file is gone and whose reviews plainly are this chapter's: at least two matching trailing segments in the same folder (a moved vault), or a review of exactly the chapter's current text, 500 characters or more (a rename or move in Obsidian). A hash match outranks a path match, more segments outrank fewer, and two candidates equal on both adopt nothing; the plan's "then newest" was dropped, since newest cannot tell two different chapters apart. `adoptReviews` rewrites the whole history into the new folder with the new `sourcePath`, removing each old file after its copy is written, through a journal (`.adoption.json`) that the next store call completes after a crash. App.tsx re-homes every review it opens to the exact path it opened, shows "Reopened the review made when this chapter was at <old path>", and the unlink and missing-chapter messages say how to get the review back.

Checked by `test/main/reviews.test.ts` (33 cases, including a synthetic 0.1.0 review in `test/fixtures/review-0.1.0.json` that loads unchanged, saves back with only the decided fields changed and rebases keeping every field) and store tests for `decidedAt`. In a `dev:clean` window with A/ and B/ copies of samples Chapter 01: an accepted manual change in A left B opening clean; an accepted change saved in B changed only B's `Get-FileHash` (A stayed `950A07DB…`); renaming B in place while open showed the new unlink card, and reopening it from the list brought its review back, moved to `chapter-01-the-deluge`, with A's review left where it was.

### C.46 The launch check reads its answers properly (`setup.4`, 2026-10-15)

C.42 had already taken the model requests out of the launch check. What remained was how it read the answers: `looksLikeAuth` called any message that mentioned "auth" a sign-in problem ("the author" included), and the check said ready whenever `initialize` answered.

`src/main/providers/claudeRuntime.ts` now runs `--version`, `auth status --json` and the `initialize` handshake side by side and hands the answers to `classifyClaude` (`setup.2`). `auth status` is believed when it says signed out; the account decides what Claude Code signs in with; a handshake that failed for a sign-in reason wins; one that timed out falls back on `auth status`. The handshake (`claudeHandshake.ts`) gets 60 s on the first check and 20 s after, and its answer serves the model list for ten minutes. `looksLikeAuth` is gone: failures go through `classifyClaudeFailure`. `claudeDiagnostics` lists what was found and how it signs in, never the email or organisation. `test/shared/policy.test.ts` keeps `child_process` in `claudeBinary.ts` alone and Claude Code's credential file, `setup-token` and `logout` out of the source.

Checked in `dev:clean` windows against the author's Claude Code 2.1.266: plain, ready (subscription), a re-check answering in about 1 s; `--claude-config-empty`, not signed in; with `--api-key-dummy` added, ready (API key).

### C.47 Error results arrive as success with is_error (`setup.5`, 2026-10-15)

Claude Code reports some failures as a `result` whose subtype says `success` while `is_error` is true; signed out, it sends `{subtype: 'success', is_error: true, result: 'Not logged in · Please run /login'}`. The old mapping took the subtype at its word on one path, and `mapSubtype` and `mapAssistantError` each worded failures their own way.

`src/main/providers/claudeResult.ts` now decides: a refusal first, then both shapes through `classifyClaudeFailure` (`setup.2`), with Claude Code's own words kept in `detail`; a run with no result at all goes through the same classifier on its stderr. `mapSubtype` and `mapAssistantError` are gone, and `mapThrown` went with the SDK at `cli.1`. Start-up failure reasons (`cli_version_too_old` and the rest) are recognised anywhere in a result: Claude Code 2.1.266 does not send them, and the field a later version uses is not documented. In the renderer, `useAi` keeps the last failure through `nextLastFailure`, the status bar reads it, and a review that fails because Claude Code is missing, too old, signed out or its sign-in ended re-checks and opens Setup.

Checked by `test/main/claudeResult.test.ts`, and in a `dev:clean` window with `--claude-config-empty`: `ai:review` returned `claude-not-signed-in` with "Not logged in · Please run /login" as its detail.

### C.48 The scrollers have opaque backgrounds (LCD text while scrolling) (`theme.1`, 2026-10-16)

The author saw text turn heavier while scrolling, then sharpen (11.0, idea 18). Four of the elements that scroll had no background of their own: `.center` (the manuscript), `.body--single` (which holds the Setup screen), `.setup` and `.rows` (the suggestions list). At this machine's 96 DPI, Chromium gives a scroller a composited layer of its own, with LCD (ClearType) text on it, only when it can tell the scroller is opaque.

Measured on 2026-10-14 with the project's Electron 44.3.0 on a probe page (200 paragraphs of 17 px Georgia, 25 DevTools wheel steps of 400 px, a trace with `disabled-by-default-cc.debug`): a transparent scroller got no layer of its own and the window was re-rasterised as it moved, 153 RasterTask events; with `background: #f7f5f1` it got an `OverflowScrolling` layer whose contents layer had `contents_opaque=true` and `lcd_text_disallowed_reason: none`, and the same scroll took 41 RasterTask events.

Every scroller now has an opaque background in a colour token: `--bg` on `.center`, `.body--single` and `.setup`, and `--panel` on the side panel's scrollers after `panel.1` (C.49): `.rows`, `.side__lower` and `.side__body`. The colours equal what showed through before. `test/renderer/scrollers.test.ts` fails when a rule that scrolls has no `var(--…)` background (since `theme.4`, these checks are in `test/renderer/themeCss.test.ts`). `state/scroll.ts` does not round its scroll positions, because Chromium already snaps `scrollTop`.

Measured again in the app on 2026-10-16: a `dev:clean` window, viewport 1424x901 at 100%, a review with 115 suggestions and 15 comments, `LayerTree.compositingReasons`, then 25 wheel steps of 400 px over each scroller traced with `['cc', 'disabled-by-default-cc.debug']` after `Page.bringToFront`.

- **Before.** `.center` had no `OverflowScrolling` layer, only one promoted for `Overlap`, which was repainted as it scrolled: 192 RasterTask events, and its LCD reason switched between `none` (45 snapshots) and `background-color-not-opaque` (50). That switch between two kinds of antialiasing is a likely cause of what the author saw. The suggestions list sat on an `Overlap` layer with `background-color-not-opaque` throughout, so its text was always greyscale.
- **After.** `.center` has an `OverflowScrolling` layer and a contents layer with `contents_opaque=true` and `none` in all 29 snapshots; the same scroll took 31 RasterTask events. The list region and the details region each have their own `OverflowScrolling` layer, contents opaque, `none` throughout, and 0 to 2 RasterTask events. At 200%, where the short panel scrolls as one, `.side__body` has the same: an `OverflowScrolling` layer, contents opaque, `none` in all 32 snapshots. The list's text is now LCD at rest as well.

Floating cards over a scroller (the notification cards, the lock card, the tooltip and the edit popover) get layers of their own with `background-color-not-opaque`, so their text is greyscale-antialiased. That was seen on the probe page on 2026-10-14 and again for a notification card in the app on 2026-10-16 (the `.notes` layer), and is accepted.

Whether the effect the author saw is gone is for his eyes: in sitting C (`theme.10` b) he scrolls with `.center`'s background set to `transparent` in DevTools and back, and the result (gone, reduced or unchanged) is recorded here.

### C.49 The side panel scrolls in two regions, or as one in a short window (`panel.1`, 2026-10-16)

The side panel scrolled as a whole and the suggestions list inside it scrolled too (`max-height: 42vh`), so two scrollbars sat side by side, and stepping through a long list moved the focused suggestion's details off screen.

`.side` no longer scrolls: it is `overflow: clip`, so script cannot scroll it either, and `min-height: 0`, which keeps its grid row to the window, as `clip` does not make it a scroll container. Inside it, `.side__body` is a flex column with two regions that each scroll. The list (`.side__list`) keeps its heading pinned and takes what it needs, up to 55% of the height; `.side__lower` below it holds the details, comments, resolved comments, Unanchored and History.

**Following the focus.** When the focus moves, by key or by click, `followFocus` in `state/scroll.ts` keeps the suggestion in view with the app's own timing, instead of `scrollIntoView`, which also scrolls ancestors that are not meant to scroll.

- **The row** is scrolled with `if-needed` (`shared/ui/scroll.ts`): a row already wholly in view stays where it is, and one partly or wholly out of view comes in with a 24 px margin, so j and k still show a row of context. As first landed it used `nearest`, which also moves a row that is in view but within 24 px of an edge: a click on the first or last visible row slid the list by about a row, and a click on that row's tick then accepted its neighbour.
- **The details.** With two regions, the Focused suggestion section is brought back into `.side__lower` the same way, so the details return when the author has scrolled that region down to read comments or History and then moves on; as first landed they stayed off screen. With one scroller only the row is followed: `.side__body` then holds both, and a second scroll would cancel the first.
- **Tall details.** A section taller than its region keeps its top in view, so a long suggestion's reason and Edit button can sit below the fold until the region is scrolled. For a box that fits the view only without the 24 px margins, `nearest` and `if-needed` shrink them, so a section a few pixels shorter than its region shows whole.
- **Quick keys.** Whether a box needs a scroll is judged where a scroll in flight will land (`retarget`), not where it is mid-way, so a row in view now that the landing would carry away still gets its scroll.

**Short windows.** The panel scrolls as one when the window is less than 540 CSS pixels high inside: `@media (height < 540px)` makes `.side__body` the one scroller and lets the list run to its full length. Zoom changes that height; a chapter does not. The first version used a container query on the panel's own height, below 420 px (the plan said about 480), and that height changes from chapter to chapter: the filter chips take one row for 3 categories, two for 7 and three for 13 (36, 65 and 94 px in a 900x600 window at 100%), and the status bar takes two rows (51 px instead of 32) once it shows the save time, provider and model, effort, cost and a rate-limit notice. So the layout switched between chapters in the same window: panels of 451, 422 and 393 px in a 900x600 window at 100% (two regions, two, one), and 442, 413 and 384 px in a 1600x1000 window at 175% (two, one, one). The figures 420 was chosen from, 461 and 490 px at 900x600 and 312 to 342 px at 1440x940 and 200%, came from viewports of those sizes set with `Emulation.setDeviceMetricsOverride`, not from windows: the window frame takes 16 by 39 px here, so a 900x600 window, the smallest the app allows, is 884x561 inside.

Measured on 2026-10-16 in `dev:clean` windows sized from outside with `SetWindowPos` (outer sizes; `GetClientRect` confirmed the inside), on Chapter 02 (3 categories), a long sample (7) and Chapter 01 with its suggestions spread over all 13, each with and without the status bar's extra items:

- **900x600** (561 px inside): two regions at 100%, panel 374 to 451 px; one scroller from 110% (510 px).
- **1440x940**, the default (901 px): two regions up to 150% (601 px; panel 445 to 493 px); one scroller at 175% (515 px; 330 to 407) and 200% (450 px; 224 to 322).
- **1280x800** (761 px): two regions at 125% (609 px), one scroller at 150% (507 px).
- **1600x1000 at 175%** (549 px): two regions, panel 364 to 442 px. 1600x985 (540 px) still has two, 1600x983 (539 px) one.

Every chapter got the same layout in each of these. 540 sits 21 px below the smallest window at 100% and 25 px above the default window at 175%. The smallest two-region panel measured was 356 px (13 categories and a two-row status bar, 540 px inside); in the smallest window at 100% it is 374 px, a list region of 206 px (about eight rows) and a details region of 168 px. The Focused suggestion section is 132 px with a one-line reason and 183 px with a three-line one, so in that window the latter needs a short scroll of its region to show the Edit button.

Checked first in a `dev:clean` window at 900x600 and 1440x940 viewports, at 100% and 200%, with 3 and 115 suggestions and 0 and 15 comments: 16 states, each stepped 45 rows down with j and 20 back with k where the list was long enough, with the panel measured every 5 steps and screenshots after opening, after j and after k, all looked at. No scroller was ever inside another or beside it, and the focused row was in view at every check. Those runs started with the details region at its top, so they did not catch the details staying off screen once it was scrolled.

Checked again after the review fixes, in a window sized as above, on the long sample (169 suggestions, 15 comments). At 1440x940 and 900x600 at 100%, 1600x985 at 175%, 1440x940 at 200% and 900x600 at 110%, the lower region was wheeled 1200 px down, then 45 j and 20 k were pressed 70 ms apart (25 ms apart as well at the first two sizes), with a second wheel before the k: at every fifth key the focused row was wholly in view and, with two regions, the details section was back in view, the region at 0 after the first j. Clicking the first and then the last wholly visible row, at 1440x940 and 900x600 at 100% (list at 88 px) and 1440x940 at 200% (panel at 300 px), left the scroll position unchanged with the clicked row still under the pointer, and at 1440x940 a click on the row's tick accepted that row; before the fix the first click moved the list from 88 to 64 px. A suggestion of 170 characters with a long reason gave a 376 px section in a 190 px region: heading and top in view, the rest below the fold. Screenshots of each chapter at 900x600 and 100% and at 1600x1000 and 175% were looked at.

### C.50 The theme rides on nativeTheme, not on a data attribute (`theme.5`, 2026-10-16)

A web page usually switches themes with a `data-theme` attribute on `<html>`, set by script, and the dark colours under `[data-theme='dark']`. The app does not. The main process sets Electron's `nativeTheme.themeSource` to the Appearance setting (`system`, `light` or `dark`; `src/main/theme.ts`), and `app.css` keeps its dark tokens inside `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)`. That one setting drives the three things that have to agree:

- **The page.** `themeSource` decides what `prefers-color-scheme` reports in the renderer, so the stylesheet picks its block with no script, before React has run. Each block's `color-scheme`, and `<meta name="color-scheme" content="light dark">` before the stylesheet loads, give the scrollbars, select lists and form fields the same theme.
- **The window.** `nativeTheme.shouldUseDarkColors` follows `themeSource`, and the computer's mode under `system`, so `currentWindowBackground()` hands `createWindow` the matching `--bg` (`WINDOW_BACKGROUND`, which a test pins to both blocks), and `installTheme` repaints every window when Windows switches mode.
- **The title bar.** On Windows, Electron sets the window's `DWMWA_USE_IMMERSIVE_DARK_MODE` attribute from the same source, so the frame matches with no code of the app's own.

Main sets `themeSource` from settings before the window exists, and the window stays hidden until its first paint (C.36), so no frame shows in the wrong theme. A data attribute could only be set once the page's script ran: until then the page would follow the computer, and the window background and title bar would each need handling of their own. A changed setting applies at once: `settings:set` calls `applyTheme`, which does nothing when that theme is already in effect.

Checked on 2026-10-16 in a `dev:clean` window on a scratch userData with `"theme": "dark"`: `prefers-color-scheme: dark` matched, the body was `rgb(28, 26, 23)`, and `DwmGetWindowAttribute(hwnd, 20)` on the app's window returned 1. After `settings:set {theme: 'light'}` the media query no longer matched, the body was `rgb(247, 245, 241)` and the attribute 0; `system` gave the same with Windows in light mode, and `dark` gave 1 again. The Windows setting was read, never changed.

### C.51 A rewritten saved suggestion is a change against the saved words (`apply.22`, 2026-10-16)

7.4 said only that rewriting a suggestion updates `replacement`. That is right until the suggestion has been saved into the chapter. After a save the base holds the replacement (`baseSide: 'replacement'`), and compose copies the base whenever the side a hunk wants is the side the base holds, so new replacement words reached the page, the side panel and the tooltip but never the file: `unsavedDecisionCount` stayed 0, and Save to chapter said "Saved to the chapter file." and left the old words in place. The other places that change words were already safe: a paragraph edit makes manual hunks over the base, and a rebase moves a hunk onto the side it finds.

A hunk has two texts, and the base must hold one of them, so a saved suggestion cannot keep the model's original, show the saved words and offer new ones all at once. `editSuggestion` now takes the base text and keeps what is in the file: the saved words become `original` and the base side, and the new words the replacement. The model's first words are given up. The file no longer holds them, the backup made before the save that replaced them does (6.5), and the alternative, a third text on every hunk that compose, rebase and commit would all have to carry, was more machinery than an undo to words already overwritten is worth. "Accept now" unticked used to send a saved suggestion back to pending with its model original, so Save would have put that original back; now the rewrite waits as a pending suggestion against the saved words, the page reads as the file, and Save writes nothing for it. A saved change the author had turned back (pending or dismissed, so that Save would restore the original) is treated the same way, so the page shows the saved words again: the one case where a rewrite unticked changes what the page reads.

Words that read exactly as the file does change nothing, so both sides become the file's words and `baseSide` follows the decision at that moment. That alone was not enough: the next ordinary decision moved `desiredSide` off `baseSide` again, and a hunk reading "begun → begun" counted as one unsaved change while compose still gave the base, so a click on the word or a dismiss brought "Save 1 change to chapter", a leave prompt and a History count, and Save answered "The chapter already matches the page." (the review of `apply.22`). So a hunk whose two sides are the same never counts as unsaved, whatever its decision (`changesBase`), and compose, commit and the page's live layout copy the base for it (`decidedText`), which keeps compose and the count in step even if such a hunk drifts after a fuzzy rebase: rewriting a saved suggestion and then back to the saved words leaves nothing to save, and so does whatever the author decides on it afterwards; a suggestion never saved but rewritten to the file's own words no longer counts as one unsaved change that would write nothing. Such a hunk reads "started → started" in the list, under a grey "was started" ghost on the page. Rewriting a saved change that was turned back to exactly its saved words keeps its `original`, which is still what turning it back restores. A stale hunk has no place in the base and only takes the new words.

### C.52 Deletions and insertions are placed by what surrounds them (`apply.23`, 2026-10-17)

A deletion has no text on one side, and neither has an insertion. `locate` searched only for text, so such a hunk was placed by its other side, and next to an identical word that went wrong both ways (the `apply.3` review). A saved deletion of one word of "the the frayed" (accepted, then Save to chapter, so `baseSide: 'replacement'` and zero width) came back after any change on disk as a suggestion to delete the "the" that remained, or another "the": the words it had deleted were the only text to search for, and C.5 let the disk win on any match. Accepting and saving would have deleted that word too. The other way round, an accepted deletion not saved yet but already made in the file was placed on the "the" that remained, and Save deleted it ("and frayed end"). Both were seen in a `dev:clean` window. Insertions had the same fault: a suggestion to insert "the " was taken as made, and accepted, on whichever "the" in the chapter had the best surroundings, and turning it back would have removed that word.

What `rebase.ts` does now:

- **An empty side is a place.** For a side that holds no text, `placeCandidates` looks for the text that surrounded the hunk in the old file: 48 characters either side together, then 24 and 12, then either side alone at those widths, so an edit right next to the place still leaves one side to find it by. A find inside a word is no find, since deletions and insertions are whole words. Each place is scored like a word, by how much of the 40 characters either side still reads as before, so the place a doubled word was taken out of, whose surroundings agree on both sides (80), outranks its twin, which agrees on one side at most (41 in the sample chapter).
- **The edges of the file count.** Where both texts begin, or end, within the 40 characters and agree all the way, that side agrees in full (`agreeBefore`, `agreeAfter`); where only one of them begins there, it agrees no further. Without this nothing within 40 characters of the start could agree in full, and an accepted insertion at the very start of a chapter went stale on any change.
- **Ties keep the decision.** Where the surroundings agree as well, the side the base holds now comes before distance (C.6 had distance first). Around a doubled word, the words and the place beside them often agree exactly as well, and distance, measured from the old offset, favoured whichever of them text removed above happened to shift towards.
- **The disk wins only plainly.** A candidate on the other side, which flips the decision (C.5), counts only where it plainly is the change made or undone there (`plainlyOnDisk`): a place whose surroundings read as before in full on one side, or for at least 12 characters of whole words on both, a side with less old text than 12 characters saying nothing (a place found only by "before", the last word of the file, recurring further up had 1 and 6 characters and outranked the deletion's own words by one, the `apply.23` review); words whose surroundings read as before on both sides; on one side, unless the words stood right beside a copy of themselves, since a doubled word's twin may then be all that is left of them; or, for a hunk whose own side has words, the only such words in the file, as before. A side counts only where the old file has text on it: the start of the file alone says nothing, so "Well, it rained." added at the top is not taken for a deleted "Well, " put back. When a candidate that is not plain ranks best, the hunk's own side is most likely gone from its place, so the hunk goes stale rather than fall back on a weaker match of its own side. This is the task's "never flip an accepted hunk with `baseSide: 'replacement'` to pending on an `ambiguous` match of `original` (leave it, or make it stale)", applied to every flip in both directions, with stale as the answer; a match its surroundings set apart is not taken as ambiguous, so a deleted word put back where it was still comes back as a suggestion.
- **A place is weighed against copies of the words near it** (`settleCopies`, the `apply.23` review). A place found by the text on one side can sit right beside the hunk's own words, or a word or two from them, and its score on the other side then runs into them or into whatever was typed beside them. The first version counted those letters wherever they happened to match the old text next to the hunk, so an edited neighbour let the place beat the words by a character or two: with "before the the storm" made "after the the storm", a suggested deletion of either "the" was taken as made ("1 decision changed to match the file."), and an accepted one too, so Save never deleted the word; a saved insertion became a suggestion to insert "had" beside the "had" in the file. In random edits of one or two words within eight words of a hunk whose words were left alone, that changed the side of about 1 in 15 deletions and saved insertions. Now every copy of the words within 40 characters of a place is weighed against it on the side the copy is on, counting whole words only (`wordsBefore`, `wordsAfter`: the "e" of "the" agreeing with the "e" of "before" is chance, and so is the space before a word). For a copy right beside the place: from the place, how far the new text reads as the old with the words taken out, against as the old with them in. For a copy further off: how far the text beyond the copy reads as the old text beyond the hunk, against the place's own score on that side. A copy that reads further takes the place away; a place that reads further against every copy stands, and a copy right beside it is the twin of a doubled word, which leaves the pool. A tie right beside the place counts for the place when the words stood twice in a row and stand once there now ("and the the frayed" made "but the frayed"), since the change is made whichever copy went; a tie further off counts for it when a copy right beside it lost; a copy whose own surroundings read further than the place's in all is no tie. Any other tie removes the place and keeps the words, but as no place for Save to write a decision: "river the before" made "night the to before" reads as well with "the" kept as with "the" taken out and "night the to" typed.
- **A place found on one side gives way to plain words.** An empty place the base holds, found by its surroundings on one side only, gives way to the other side's words found plainly: an insertion made by hand, with the word before it changed or another word typed after it, is taken as made rather than as one still to make, which Save would have written a second time.
- **Save writes only at a plain place.** A decision Save would write (an accepted change not saved yet, or a saved one turned back) stays on its own side only where that side is plainly in place (`placedPlainly`): words with one side of their surroundings as before, or the only such words when they did not stand beside a copy of themselves, and not tied with a place beside them; an empty place with one side as before in full and the other for at least 12 characters of whole words. Otherwise the hunk goes stale and nothing is written for it, as when a doubled word was deleted by hand together with the words either side of it and only its twin is left, or another word was typed right at the place of an accepted insertion ("Then later Mara", where the text before agrees for none). The first version asked an empty place for both sides in full, so an accepted insertion went to Unanchored after any edit within 40 characters on either side, "steps" made "stairs" 15 characters after it among them, and Save then wrote nothing for it (the `apply.23` review).
- **No place by a guess.** Only words are placed by the fuzzy search. A zero-width place found by surroundings that no longer read as before is a guess that can fall inside a word: a suggested insertion of "very " in "The ewes were always fine." went, after the file changed to "always good.", "quite good." and back, to "always| fine" with the "?" mark, and accepting it and saving wrote "alwaysvery  fine" (seen in a `dev:clean` window, the `apply.23` review; the old code did the same with both words changed at once, "quite gvery ood"). A deletion or an insertion with no place found exactly now goes to Unanchored, whatever its decision.
- **Stale stays stale.** A stale hunk's offsets are 0. A deletion or an insertion that went stale is not searched for again, since only its surroundings could place it: searched for around 0, it came back at the start of the chapter.
